## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time (~400-600 steps): robots should be **moving** (score +1 per stationary robot)
- After switch time: robots should be **stationary** (score +1 per moving robot)
- The green wall lights up at switch time as a signal

**Optimal strategy:**
- Phase 1: Move around (exploration/repulsion)
- Phase 2: Stop when green is detected (C=7 with color=2 for green)

**Analysis of champion (score 4931.39):**
- State 0: Repulsion (moving) → transitions on few-neighbours or gray floor
- State 1: Stop (magenta LED) → transitions on various conditions
- State 2: Stop (cyan LED) → stays stopped mostly

The champion uses two Stop states, which is interesting. The green color detection (C=7, l=2) is notably absent from the champion, yet it scores well. The pure-stop machines (score ~4957) are almost as good, meaning the arena floor is gray and robots are mostly stopping anyway.

**Key insight:** Score ~4957 for pure stop means robots moving costs more than stopping. But score 4931 beats pure stop, suggesting the champion moves before switch and stops after. The green signal (color 2) should trigger stopping.

Let me design machines that:
1. Move before switch (exploration/repulsion)
2. Detect green (C=7, l=2) and stop

**Plan:**
1. Copy champion verbatim (elitism)
2. Several exploitations of champion (small parameter tweaks)
3. Exploration with green-detection transitions
4. Simple 2-state machines with green trigger

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key insight from Round 2:**
The champion is a simple 2-state machine:
- State 0: Repulsion (moving, rep=3.0) → transitions to Stop when green detected (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) or with tiny probability (C=5, p=0.002)
- State 1: Stop → stays stopped (tiny escape probability p=0.001)

This perfectly matches the mission: move before switch, then stop when green wall appears. Score 3753.90 vs pure-stop ~4957.

**Strategy:**
- The green detection trigger (C=7, l=2) is crucial
- Repulsion keeps robots moving before switch
- Very low escape probability from stop state keeps them stopped

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Vary rep gain (2.0-5.0)
2. Vary green detection probability (0.8-0.99)
3. Vary the tiny stop-escape probability
4. Try exploration instead of repulsion
5. Add extra transitions (multiple conditions to stop)
6. Try 3-state variants with better coverage

**Exploration ideas:**
- 3-state: move → detect green → hard stop
- Different movement behaviors before switch
- Add neighbor-count conditions to help clustering

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** The new champion uses Attraction (B=4, att=2.0) instead of Repulsion. This is interesting - attraction keeps robots moving (grouped together, but still moving), and when green appears, they stop. Score 3645.35.

**Key pattern confirmed:**
- State 0: Movement behavior → stop when green detected (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) or tiny random probability
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped (tiny escape p=0.001)

**Why attraction beats repulsion?** Attraction keeps robots moving but in a more coordinated way. They cluster together, which might mean more robots are moving consistently before the switch (since clustered robots still move to maintain attraction).

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Vary attraction gain (1.0-5.0 range around 2.0)
2. Vary green detection probability (0.85-0.99)
3. Try attraction + extra transitions (neighbor count)
4. Mix attraction with repulsion in multi-state
5. Try very high green detection probability

**Exploration:**
- 3-state: attract → stop hard (two stop states for redundancy)
- Combine repulsion and attraction
- Try different movement speeds/behaviors

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Attraction with gain=4.0 beats lower gains. The trend: att=1.0→worse, att=2.0→3645, att=3.0→3740, att=4.0→3577. Higher attraction keeps robots moving more vigorously (chasing each other), so more robots are moving before the switch. When green appears, they stop.

**Key observations:**
- Higher attraction gain is better (try att=5.0, the maximum)
- Green detection probability p=0.9 seems good (p=0.95 was worse - maybe too quick to stop on false positives? Actually C=7 only fires when green IS seen, so higher should be better... but p=0.95 performed worse than p=0.9 in round 4. Maybe the randomness of the sigmoid matters)
- The tiny escape probability from stop (0.001-0.002) seems optimal
- Adding neighbor-count condition (C=3, threshold 8) as extra transition helped in repulsion case

**Exploitation:**
1. Try att=5.0 (maximum)
2. Try att=4.5
3. Vary green detection prob around 0.9 (0.88, 0.92)
4. Add extra transitions (neighbor above threshold) to attract state
5. Keep champion exact copy

**Exploration:**
- Try GoToColor (B=8) with green color (clr=2) as movement state - robots actively seek green wall, then stop

# ROUND 6/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Major breakthrough:** GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2) as the movement state scores 2833.61! This is dramatically better than attraction or repulsion. 

**Why GoToColor green works so well:**
- Before switch: robots actively move toward the green wall (but there's no green yet, so they may wander or stay near walls - actually wait, before switch there's no green wall, so B=8 with clr=2 might make them explore randomly or stay still if no green visible)
- Actually: before switch, no green is visible, so GoToColor green might behave like exploration/random movement
- After switch: green wall appears, robots rush toward it (moving!) - but wait, we WANT them to stop after switch...
- The C=7 (perceive green) transition fires when green appears, switching them to Stop state
- So: before switch = GoToColor green (moves randomly since no green), after switch = detects green → stops

The key is the transition fires WHEN green appears, before the robot reaches it. Score 2833 vs 3577 for attraction - huge improvement.

**Exploitation:**
1. Vary green detection probability (0.85-0.99)
2. Vary tiny escape probability from stop
3. Add extra transition (neighbor count) to GoToColor state
4. Try GoAwayColor green (B=9) - might also work interestingly
5. Try different clr values with GoToColor (other colors)

**Exploration:**
- GoToColor with clr=1 (red), clr=3 (blue) - maybe arena has other colors
- 3-state with GoToColor → Stop → GoToColor (to handle robots that miss green)
- GoToColor green + repulsion combo

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Score 2552.72 with GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2) → Stop on green detection (C=7, l=2, p=0.9) or tiny random (p=0.001), Stop state escapes with p=0.0005.

**Key parameters of champion:**
- Green detection transition: p=0.9 (fires 90% of time when green seen)
- Random escape from move: p=0.001 (very rarely stops randomly)
- Random escape from stop: p=0.0005 (almost never leaves stop)

**What's working:** GoToColor green is brilliant - before switch, no green visible so robot wanders/explores. When green wall lights up, robot detects it (C=7) and stops with high probability.

**Exploitation directions:**
1. Try lower escape from stop: p=0.0001 or 0.0002
2. Try higher green detection: p=0.92, 0.95, 0.99
3. Try p0x1=0.0005 (same as champion) but vary green detection
4. Add neighbor-count transition to GoToColor state
5. Try very low random escape from move state (p=0.0005)

**Exploration:**
- 3-state variant with two stop states (redundancy)
- GoToColor + extra transition checking neighbor count
- Try p=1.0 for green detection (always stop when green seen)

Note on format errors: I keep seeing errors about missing destination indices. Let me be very careful - `--n<I>x<K> D` where D is the destination index among OTHER states. With 2 states, leaving state 0, destination must be 0 (pointing to state 1). With 3 states, leaving state 1, others are [0,2] so D=0→state0, D=1→state2.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Score 2223.74. The key improvement was reducing stop-state escape probability to p=0.0001 (1e-04). 

**Clear trend:** Lower escape probability from stop state = better score. The stop state should be nearly absorbing once entered.
- p=0.001 escape: ~2552
- p=0.0005 escape: ~2396-2398
- p=0.0002 escape: ~2262
- p=0.0001 escape: ~2223 (champion)

**Next steps:** Try p=0.00005 or even p=0.0 (but 0.0 might not be valid - actually it should be fine as a real). Also the green detection probability seems best at p=0.9. The random escape from move state (p=0.001) is also important - lower might not be better since we need robots to stop eventually even if they don't see green.

**Key parameters to tune:**
1. Stop escape: try p=0.00005, p=0.00001, p=0.0 
2. Green detection: p=0.9 seems optimal, try 0.88, 0.93
3. Random escape from move: p=0.001 seems good, try p=0.0005, p=0.0008
4. Adding neighbor-count transitions to help clustering (avoid too-few-neighbors stopping)

**Important format note:** I keep getting errors about missing destination indices. With 2 states, `--n1x0 0` means "from state 1, transition 0 goes to destination index 0" which refers to the only other state (state 0). This seems correct. Let me double-check: with 2 states, from state 1, other states = [0], so D=0 → state 0. That's correct in all my machines.

The error about `--n1x1` missing destination - this only happens in 3-state machines where I wrote `--n1x1` without the destination before the condition. Let me be very careful with 3-state machines.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis:** Score 2195.58 with:
- Move escape (random stop): p=0.0008 (8e-04) - slightly lower than 0.001
- Stop escape: p=0.0001 (1e-04) - very low

**Key finding:** p=0.0008 for move-escape is better than p=0.001 or p=0.0005. The sweet spot seems to be around 0.0008. Also p=0.0 for stop escape gave 2270 (worse than 0.0001), suggesting a tiny escape is actually beneficial - perhaps a robot that never leaves stop state is penalized for some edge case.

**Parameter landscape:**
- Move escape (p0x1): 0.0005=bad, 0.0008=best, 0.001=ok, 0.0015=ok
- Stop escape (p1x0): 0.0=2270, 0.0001=2195(best), 0.0002=2262, 0.0005=2396
- Green detection (p0x0): 0.9=best, 0.88=ok, 0.93=ok

**Exploitation:**
1. Fine-tune move escape around 0.0008: try 0.0007, 0.0009, 0.00075
2. Fine-tune stop escape around 0.0001: try 0.00008, 0.00015, 0.00012
3. Combine best move escape with varying green detection
4. Try p0x1=0.0008 with green detection 0.88, 0.93

**Important format fix:** The repeated error about `--n1x1` missing destination. Looking carefully at the format: `--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C`. So `--n1x1 D` must come BEFORE `--c1x1`. In my 2-state machines with 2 transitions from state 1: `--n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 C ... --n1x1 0 --c1x1 C ...` - destination 0 is correct for 2-state machine from state 1. This should be fine. The error must be from 3-state machines where I forgot the destination. I'll avoid 3-state machines this round to be safe.

## Reasoning

**Final round - maximize exploitation of the champion.**

Champion: Score 2195.58
- B=8 GoToColor green, vel=1.0, clr=2
- Green detection: p=0.9, l=2 → stop
- Random move escape: p=0.0008
- Stop escape: p=0.0001

**What we know:**
- Move escape p=0.0008 is the sweet spot (0.0007 gave 2277, 0.0009 not tested vs champion, 0.001 gave 2318)
- Stop escape p=0.0001 is best (0.0 gave 2270, 0.0002 gave 2262, 0.00005/0.00008 not tested)
- Green detection p=0.9 is best overall (0.88, 0.85, 0.95, 0.93 all gave ~2305)

**Final round strategy - fine-grained search:**
1. Move escape: try 0.00085, 0.00080 (champion), 0.00070, 0.00065, 0.00090
2. Stop escape: try 0.00005, 0.00008, 0.0001 (champion), 0.00012
3. Green detection: 0.9 seems dominant, try 0.87, 0.91
4. Keep champion exact
5. A few with p0x1=0.0007 + varying stop escape (since 0.0007 was second best)

I'll focus entirely on the 2-state GoToColor green design since it's clearly optimal.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 10 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.45 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
